Hubble Telescope sees cosmic 'cat's tail' that could wrap around Earth and the sun 500 times

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Latest space missions, discoveries and exploration updates.

Stargazing tips, cosmic events and expert insights on the universe

Start exploring exclusive deals, expert advice and more

Space news, cosmic updates and expert insights straight to your inbox.

Breaking space news, the latest updates on rocket launches, skywatching events and more!

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Breaking space news, the latest updates on rocket launches, skywatching events and more!

Sign up to our monthly entertainment newsletter to keep up with all our coverage of the latest sci-fi and space movies, tv shows, games and books.

Discover this week's must-see night sky events, moon phases, and stunning astrophotos. Sign up for our skywatching newsletter and explore the universe with us!

Space.com's Sci-Fi Reader's Club. Read a sci-fi short story every month and join a virtual community of fellow science fiction fans!

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

"It's a testament to how much these massive planets really influence their surrounding dust and icy bodies out to hundreds of astronomical units."

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Using the Hubble Space Telescope, astronomers have investigated a cosmic cat's tail, finding it likely formed after two Pluto-sized bodies smashed together.

The feature, nicknamed the Cat's Tail, extends out from the debris disk known as Beta Pictoris which is located in a planetary system located 63 light-years away. This tail would put that of the average tabby to shame: It's so long that it could curl around the sun-Earth system about 500 times. That's about as long as 200 trillion house cats.

The major difference between our solar system and the planetary system that hosts the Cat's Tail is the latter's relative youth. While our solar system is around 4.6 billion years old, Beta Pictoris is a cosmic kitten no more than a few million years old. That means it is still host to collisions between planetary bodies similar to the ones that rocked the solar system during its infancy.

The team behind this research gained new insights into the Cat's Tail using 14 years of observations. This allowed them to solve the long-standing mystery of the violent conditions under which it formed.

"The Cat's Tail is thought to be the aftermath of a collision between large bodies — think something like Pluto," team leader Arin Avsar of the University of Arizona said in a statement. "Oddly, this feature had previously only been seen with the James Webb Space Telescope (JWST), which detects infrared light, but remained invisible at shorter wavelengths, which has puzzled the community for a while."

Comparing Beta Pictoris and our solar system, one thing rapidly becomes apparent: our cosmic neighborhood is relatively well-ordered. The planets orbit the sun with most space rocks neatly delegated into bands: the main asteroid belt between Mars and Jupiter, and the Kuiper belt out beyond the orbit of Neptune.

However, this is a stark contrast to the solar system's violent and chaotic youth. Back then, the solar system was host to an infant star surrounded by a swirling disk of gas and dust in which collisions between bodies called planetesimals — the building blocks of planets — were commonplace.

Astronomers can't look back to the solar system's infancy, so to learn more about how our cosmic backyard took shape, they study nearby young planetary systems. Systems like Beta Pictoris. That doesn't mean it has been quick to divulge its secrets, though.

"We think the Cat's Tail has eluded our observations in visible light until now because of what it's made of," Avsar explained. "It consists almost entirely of what we call organic refractory material, which is heavily carbon-based. These long chains of carbon atoms absorb visible light, so they're quite black."

This new study revealed that the feline feature is cat-like in more ways than just its shape. It's also pleasingly fluffy, Hubble observations suggest. That's because the grains that compose it are extremely porous.

"How you can get dust grains that small that are as porous as we see here no one really understands, and that's part of why we study this feature," Avsar said.

"It's possible that the velocities involved in the collisions were so high that the carbon grains vaporized, turned into gas, and then recondensed as they cooled, and that's how they became so porous," team member Kevin Wagner of the U of A said.

The scientists also discovered a hitherto undiscovered feature in the Cat's Tail — a warp that seems to be the result of an interaction between the trail of material and one of the planets in the system, Beta Pictoris b, a gas giant planet 10 times more massive than Jupiter.

"This gives us another line of evidence into how giant planets influence comets through their immense gravitational force," Avsar said. "It's a testament to how much these massive planets really influence their surrounding dust and icy bodies out to hundreds of astronomical units [one astronomical unit being the distance between Earth and the sun]."

The team's research was published last month in The Astrophysical Journal Letters.

Robert Lea is a science journalist in the U.K. whose articles have been published in Physics World, New Scientist, Astronomy Magazine, All About Space, Newsweek and ZME Science. He also writes about science communication for Elsevier and the European Journal of Physics. Rob holds a bachelor of science degree in physics and astronomy from the U.K.’s Open University. Follow him on Twitter @sciencef1rst.

Emma Chamberlain Shares Her “Objects of Affection”

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

We’ve all seen just how glorious Emma Chamberlain’s L.A. home is—but have you ever wondered what the star’s most prized possessions are inside? If you have, we’ve got answers. In a new Vogue video, we dropped in on Chamberlain at home to explore what her personal “objects of affection” are. The short answer? One-of-a-kind items she simply cannot live without—from art to fashion and more.

Chamberlain began her house tour with a few of her fashion must-haves. She can’t imagine a day, for one, without using her everyday bags—including a Prada black shoulder schlepper or a green Comme des Garçons style. “I’m emotionally attached to these two bags—they’ve been everywhere with me,” she says. Then it was onto her most-worn shoes, including a white pair of the polarizing and fabulous footwear style, Margiela Tabis.

Next, the star took us to her gallery wall to share a few of her favorite art pieces, including a self-portrait. “I absolutely hate it, but I do like the eyes,” she says. There was also the abstract wooden sculpture-slash-painting of … a butt. “I got it at an antique store,” says Chamberlain. “It balances humor and aesthetic.” Also included in the mix were family heirlooms, like paintings passed down to her from her grandmother, or works made by her father. “There’s probably 20,” she says of her dad’s creations. “For the most part, he does a lot of landscapes.”

As for some of the more unexpected objects you can find in Chamberlain’s cabinet of curiosities? A big movie poster of Napoleon Dynamite (her favorite film), a drum kit—”I’ve been practicing and making some. . . improvements,” she says—and a wigged mannequin that she calls her body double. “I purchased her as a prop for my podcast set,” says Chamberlain. “She told me she wants to be called Emma.” There are two Emmas in the Chamberlain household.

Binance Adds More Withdrawal Routes on November 3 After Security Checks

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Strict editorial policy that focuses on accuracy, relevance, and impartiality

Morbi pretium leo et nisl aliquam mollis. Quisque arcu lorem, ultricies quis pellentesque nec, ullamcorper eu odio.

TL;DR: Binance’s November 3 security-recovery notice set out a wider withdrawal restart for additional tokens and networks beginning around 04:00 UTC. Binance’s announcement is a company statement, not an independent certification that all systems are secure.

The subtle point is that a route reopening is a narrow, testable milestone. It is stronger than a vague promise of normal service, yet smaller than a comprehensive incident resolution. Customers trying to move funds should confirm the correct network at the moment they initiate a transaction, because an asset ticker alone is an incomplete withdrawal instruction.

Binance’s November 3 security-recovery notice set out a wider withdrawal restart for additional tokens and networks beginning around 04:00 UTC. The company is reopening routes progressively after its late-October hot-wallet incident rather than restoring every asset in one operation. Some supported routes run over Ethereum, BNB Smart Chain, Solana, XRP Ledger and other networks, with availability determined by the exact token-and-chain combination. The dated original disclosure is the starting point for the figures and product scope discussed here.

A customer withdrawal has several moving parts: the platform must hold the asset, approve the destination route, sign the transaction safely and be able to reconcile the result. A token can exist on multiple chains, so announcing a network reopening does not necessarily make every representation of that asset withdrawable. Binance’s route lists and live withdrawal interface, rather than a broad press headline, are the practical source for supported combinations.

The update matters because access to customer assets is the operational test after a security event. Replenishing balances is one question; returning funds through secure, reliable systems is another. A phased process may be frustrating to users, but it allows the operator to validate infrastructure and monitor transactions before widening access. It is also why reported recovery progress should not be confused with universal service restoration.

A crypto incident produces several separate timelines: containment, investigation, recovery and a return to reliable service. Those phases can overlap without finishing together. A traced wallet is not automatically frozen; a frozen balance is not automatically returned; and a reopened withdrawal route is not a completed security audit. Keeping those differences visible helps readers judge progress more accurately.

This development also connects to two subjects our newsroom has been following: Binance’s earlier staged withdrawal restart and Bitcoin payment infrastructure and node software context. They explain related market structure and should not be mistaken for evidence that every new proposal is already live.

Binance’s announcement is a company statement, not an independent certification that all systems are secure. It does not mean every customer, jurisdiction or network has identical withdrawal access. Users should ignore unsolicited messages claiming to fast-track withdrawals and use only the official app or website. Transaction fees, identity checks and asset-specific restrictions may still apply.

Operational controls matter most when a business is under stress. Security announcements should identify the system affected, the customers who can act, the evidence supporting any recovery number and what remains uncertain. Clear scope protects users from false reassurance and from unnecessary panic. It also provides a sensible basis for follow-up accountability.

Look for updated token-and-network lists, completed withdrawal confirmations and explanations of any routes that remain restricted. The quality of incident follow-up will also depend on technical disclosures and the effectiveness of the company’s controls after reopening. For now, the support notice is best read as a dated expansion of eligible routes, not an all-clear for the entire platform.

Review: Zero Parades: Director's Cut (Switch 2) - ZA/UM Returns With Another Atmospheric, Cerebral Yet Overly Familiar RPG

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

How does a studio follow an era-defining masterpiece without the original creative forces behind said masterpiece? The answer, for better or worse, is to emulate the experience almost completely.

ZA/UM emerges from a controversy far too storied to cover here with a follow-up to existential crisis simulator Disco Elysium. Zero Parades is once again about a lone figure of questionable authority and skill, wandering a political melting pot of a city and trying to find salvation or oblivion. It’s achingly beautiful and constantly thought-provoking, but also very familiar for fans of Harry DuBois’ misadventures in Revachol.

The complex and multi-branching story follows Hershel Wilk — whose spy name is the distinctly cooler-sounding CASCADE — as she is reactivated after being put out in the cold following a botched mission. She awakens in her apartment with a hazy memory and her partner, Psuedopod, passed out in a chair. The meat of the story has CASCADE try to find out why her partner is in a comatose state and what exactly the Operant, her shadowy handlers, want her for.

Hershel/CASCADE ventures out into the bustling streets of Portofiro, an ideological morass that struggles and rebels under the watchful eyes of multiple factions. The societal clock has been reset to zero following the Latour-Woolgar Treaty and CASCADE’s handlers work intelligence for the ruling communist power of the Superbloc. Meanwhile, propaganda and word-of-mouth for techno-fascist group La Luz permeates every conversation she has.

ZA/UM doesn’t hold your hand in a narrative sense; the world around you is a complete mystery to the player and in most cases, the protagonist. You are left to glean an emerging plot from colourful NPCs around the city.

Not a traditional mainstream RPG experience by any stretch, the game plays out more like an investigative tabletop adventure. You navigate dialogue trees, while passive and active skill checks determine when and where information becomes available.

There’s a character build system of sorts that gives you relative freedom in what kind of amnesiac spy you wish to be. Yellow skills govern how personable and empathetic you are, pink focus on physical spycraft, and purple are technical aptitudes.

There are three status meters to balance throughout the game: fatigue, delirium, and anxiety. Actions and poor conversation choices have a direct impact on these levels, and maxing them out will force a downgrade of a corresponding colour of skills. These status effects can be managed by successful dialogue rolls, consumables, and several environmental interactions. Keeping one eye on these levels adds a layer of tension to the relatively action-free investigations.

Conditioning offers a further way to boost CASCADE's core skills. Throughout her wandering, our confused spy will discover certain thought patterns that she can condition, which in turn buffs a cluster of her abilities. These thoughts have specific unlock criteria that, much like everything else in the game, are never obvious. Unlocking them, however, adds an element of intrigue and abstract progression to your character build.

Another way to boost certain skills is by assembling an aesthetically ridiculous outfit from items picked across the city. Your skills are also vital in several decision-making encounters that occur throughout the story. These multiple-choice scenarios usually require a series of rolls and give your status levels a beating. Dramatic encounters are the closest the game gets to action and they contrast just enough with the rest of the gameplay to be exciting.

Navigating Portofiro is a smooth experience, falling somewhere between point-and-click-style pixel hunting and free exploration. ZA/UM has made incremental traversal and UI improvements over Disco Elysium that have really paid off. Movement and investigation are intuitive, and exploration is decidedly less laborious than in the previous game. There is still a lot of aimless wandering as you figure out the next objective, but a helpful map and scanning system keeps things moving at a healthy clip.

The city itself is dripping with atmosphere, thanks in no small part to the beautifully haunting score from Fernando Cabrero. The rogues and grotesques that our anti-hero interacts with are as meticulously designed as their dialogue, and the voice acting is superb (though CASCADE's inner monologue can get a bit jarring). It's one of those games where you can't wait to meet the next NPC and engage in a philosophical battle of wills and/or idiotic argument about what it means to be 'cool'.

Yet, as engrossing as this spy-out-in-the-cold odyssey is, returning fans will feel a pervading sense that they've been here before. Those that laud ZA/UM's debut opus may be clamouring for more political events set in a European dystopia, but they won't get much else besides that. Newcomers, on the other hand, are in for an engrossing, at times mind-bending treat and should certainly follow up with the superior predecessor once they're done here.

The Switch 2 version of Zero Parades is jam-packed with new content and native console features. Noteworthy is the inclusion of mouse controls, which work well and are much better suited to environment interaction than the regular controller setup. Alongside mouse controls, touchscreen support, and HD Rumble 2, this Director's Cut version of the game arrives with new outfits, a behind-the-scenes Backstage mode, and a reported 40,000 additional words in the script.

One downside is the 30fps target, which noticeably mars the experience of wandering the populated streets of the city on a large screen. The isometric perspective and UI-heavy gameplay feel much better to play undocked. It just feels baffling that 60fps was not possible, given the hugely demanding games the console can run at that level.

Going some way to balancing out the performance shortcoming is a generous suite of accessibility options, including colour balancing, contrast and dyslexia settings.

Fans who patiently awaited the next game from the Disco Elysium studio can rest easy. Zero Parades is another complex, intelligent and gorgeously presented RPG. It oozes style and boasts an engaging narrative and cast.

That said, it brings nothing new to the groundbreaking experience offered by its predecessor.

Poll: Vote for Your PS5 Game of the Month (October 2026)

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Ah, October, otherwise known as the month before GTA 6.

Indeed, it was an incredibly busy 31 days for new PS5 releases, following on from an equally manic September — and that's going to make this latest Game of the Month poll all the more intriguing.

October's most high profile title arguably launched just a few days ago: Phantom Blade Zero, the PS5 console exclusive that had been wowing onlookers for years with its gameplay trailers, finally arrived on the 29th.

The Chinese action game earned extremely impressive review scores, and it seems to have been a big hit with fans of the genre.

Meanwhile, Call of Duty: Modern Warfare 4 actually managed to deliver what many are calling the series' best campaign in years, and Castlevania: Belmont's Curse revived Konami's iconic franchise in some style.

Other standouts included the eye-watering Star Wars: Galactic Racer — a fantastic racer that's gone down far better than anyone thought it would — and Final Fantasy Resonance, which turned out to be a brilliant callback to the property's traditional JRPG roots.

Ace Combat 8: Wings of Theve deserves a shoutout too; another super highly rated release, and quite possibly the most engaging Ace Combat game of the series' modern era.

Then you've got the more divisive stuff that some of you are bound to vote for: both Stupid Never Dies and Hellraiser: Revival struggled to capture the hearts of critics, but they're the kind of games where if you enjoy them, you probably really enjoy them.

But as always, only one of these titles can be crowned Game of the Month. Before we get to the all-important vote, though, here's a quick recap of our 2026 winners so far...

Daniel Craig's Early-Career Miniseries Is A Masterpiece Every British TV Fan Has To Watch

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

We may receive a commission on purchases made from links.

When I saw a 24-year-old Daniel Craig play the sadistic South African Police Sergeant Jaapie Botha in John G. Avildsen's 1940s Apartheid drama "The Power of One," I can't say that I had him pegged for the next James Bond, but I did know that he was a remarkably hissable villain. The film quickly faded from memory, but every time Craig turned up in a movie during the 1990s, I'd remember how effective he was as Botha, and wonder why he wasn't getting meatier roles.

What I didn't know at the time was that he'd given a tour de force performance in the 1996 BBC drama serial "Our Friends in the North." The nine-part series spans 31 years in the lives of four friends from Newcastle upon Tyne, and boosted the careers of Craig, Mark Strong, Christopher Eccleston and Gina McKee. But no one demonstrated greater range than Craig, whose character, Geordie, flees an abusive, alcoholic father to pursue an unpromising life in low-level crime.

British television wasn't immediately accessible in the United States in 1996 (unless it was a pop cultural sensation like "The Office" or "Little Britain"), so I didn't find out about "Our Friends in the North" until a decade later, directly after Daniel Craig had rejuvenated the James Bond franchise in "Casino Royale." I can understand why it didn't leap over the pond. The historical references are specific and steeped in British politics, which, to American viewers, makes it somewhat daunting. But Peter Flannery's writing and the dynamite acting ensemble will hook you just as sharply as David Simon plunged you into the Baltimore social stratum with "The Wire."

Unfortunately, "Our Friends in the North" isn't readily available in the United States on streaming, but if you've got a region-free Blu-ray player you can purchase it for $33.00 on Amazon, and it's worth it. I grew up watching British crime movies like "The Long Good Friday," "Mona Lisa," and "The Krays," and Flannery's writing doesn't suffer fools. But his dialogue is so crisp and layered and immaculately performed that you feel compelled to understand it. You're thrust into a society that, from the corrupt parliament down to the alcoholic, homeless dregs, is foreign yet familiar.

Craig's Geordie faces the most dire swings of fortune. He's not a bad person, but, like so many people who choose a criminal lifestyle and succumb to substance abuse, he makes so many compromises that there's eventually no way out. It's a harrowing journey and I wish I could tell you it ends well for Geordie, but "Our Friends in the North" is not out to comfort.

AI Movie ‘Gods Don’t Give Gifts’ Gets Serious with AI-Generated Grimes Cameo, Times Square Marketing Push, Oscar Screenings

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

When we first wrote about the coming release of “Gods Don’t Give Gifts,” the first fully AI-generated feature film being self-distributed into movie theaters in the U.S., it would’ve been easy to write the whole thing off as a stunt.

Coming from YouTuber Gossip Goblin, who has an impressive and loyal following, “Gods Don’t Give Gifts” started earning comps from the press (and from the producers themselves) to the success Markiplier found when he self-released his film “Iron Lung” and made $50 million worldwide in the process. Would that success translate for an AI movie? A recent example from China suggests that may not be the case. Related Stories To Follow Her Best Picture Win, Siân Heder Opted to Make ‘the Hardest Story She Could Imagine’ How to Make IndieWire a Preferred Source on Google

It’s clear though the team behind “Gods Don’t Give Gifts” is treating this like a real project that needs to be seen in theaters and is worthy of serious critical consideration, and they’re doing that with real marketing dollars behind its theatrical push.

Today, Gossip Goblin announced that pop star Grimes is set to make a cameo in his film “Gods Don’t Give Gifts,” as is YouTuber Mike Majlak, and their involvement is being revealed via a massive Times Square ad campaign that is also launching today, all ahead of the film’s release next month on December 4. Gossip Goblin is even launching a contest for people to use AI to create their own short films within the universe of “Gods Don’t Give Gifts” that can net the winning creator $15,000.

“Gods Don’t Give Gifts” also sparked some headlines in the last few months with the news that the producers are plotting an Oscar campaign for Best Animated Feature. Turns out some FYC screenings have already started and a digital campaign begins at the end of November. All of the above suggests some real belief in the project, and with a collective 16 million views of the trailer thus far, they have reason to believe.

“We’ve been heartened already by the reaction,” producer Edward Saatchi of Fable Studios said in a statement. “One Oscar-Nominated animated film director compared ‘Gods Don’t Give Gifts’ after seeing it to ‘2001,’ and we’ve had great feedback from Pixar folks (part of Fable’s team is ex-Pixar and we hugely respect their work).”

Zack London, the man behind the moniker Gossip Goblin, in speaking with IndieWire ahead of the announcement, said he’s “fully cognizant” of the controversy around putting a movie made with AI into theaters, and he acknowledges that all of this is “a big, unproven hypothesis.” But he says it would be “disingenuous to write it all off” as just an AI movie or not a real project.

Getting screens is easy, but the real battle is making people believe there are actually humans behind this movie, even if the images are all generated by AI. Roughly 30 to 35 people are credited in the making of “Gods Don’t Give Gifts,” and a behind-the-scenes documentary that is also in the works is aimed to humanize the process of making the movie. It includes interviews with the film’s composer and voice actors and shows its Foley guy doing real post-production work. Even the act of getting a real person like Grimes in the film — whose image is generated to resemble her but who does real voice acting — required an elaborate process of photo mashing atop existing character design to fit her into the movie, as well as contacting the model makers themselves to remove built-in censors for generating images of a public figure’s likeness.

On one hand, “Gods Don’t Give Gifts” can be viewed as a scrappy indie made by a team of creatives, not just a sole prompter working alone, making a movie that wouldn’t exist otherwise. On the other hand, many would argue that the visuals on display would normally require a team of thousands to bring to life along with millions of dollars. Depending on where you fall on that spectrum may determine whether you also buy a ticket to see it.

But London and Saatchi have grander ambitions with “Gods Don’t Give Gifts” and AI filmmaking beyond just what it can do at the box office. Saatchi says that for as many great CG animated films have been released in the last 30 years since “Toy Story” first hit theaters, they’ve arguably all looked similar and been made with the same techniques. He views “Gods Don’t Give Gifts” as the next “Toy Story” moment in animation, with a movie that aims to look psychedelic and otherworldly, something that looks unique and looks like it could only be the product of AI generative models. Other films may use AI to create something cheaply that looks like a Pixar movie, but London wanted to make something that looked like nothing else.

“Pixar set the look for animated films in the ’90s — they didn’t use technology to more cheaply make a film that looked like ‘Beauty and the Beast,’ they created a radically new aesthetic with ‘Toy Story,'” Saatchi said. “That’s what ‘Gods Don’t Give Gifts’ represents: a wild new aesthetic for animated films that will define much of animated, actor-voiced films in the coming decades. This is that ‘Toy Story’ moment for our generation — a radical departure for animated films — you only need to see Grimes’ brilliant performance in the film to understand how much things are about to change for animated movies. And we hope the Academy will recognize that a film that is seminal (the first AI film), wildly technically novel and most importantly good deserves a nomination.”

The Best Gifts for Music Lovers, According to Our Editors

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

If you purchase an independently reviewed product or service through a link on our website, Rolling Stone may receive an affiliate commission.

Shopping for music lovers isn’t an easy task. Music obsessives have a penchant for snobbery, which can make them some of the most difficult giftees to shop for come holiday season. Luckily for us here at Rolling Stone, music lovers are everywhere. And luckily for everyone else, we also know how to find the best gifts for music lovers.

To help find the best gifts, we had our staff of music-obsessed shopping editors round up gifts that any music nerd in your life will not just appreciate this holiday, but actually use throughout 2027 and beyond. These run the gamut from affordable custom wall art and earplugs to the latest and greatest headphones if you’re really looking to treat the music lover in your life.

Ahead are the best gifts for music lovers during the 2026 holiday season.

One of the safest gift ideas for music lovers (and almost anyone else, for that matter) is a gift card to StubHub, which will let them see the show of their choice for less. You can go low to help offset the ludicrous ticket prices we’re now used to, or go high if you really want to cover a whole show.

Earplugs are a must for anyone attending shows regularly, and Loop has become the industry go-to. Options like these Experience 2 earplugs work to lower the volume of live music while retaining all the detail, keeping ears safe while letting the wearer enjoy the show as normal.

DJ controllers are the new guitars. If you have an aspiring DJ on your list — or anyone who likes to host parties — they’ll be thrilled to unwrap this Pioneer DJ DDJ-FLX4. The entry-level controller is a great introduction to the technology, and it’s relatively affordable as far as music gear goes.

This Is Your Brain on Music: The Science of a Human Obsession has been out for two decades, but it’s still one of the best reads for anyone interested in the art. Written by neuroscientist, musician, and record producer Daniel J. Levitin, the book explores how we hear music and why it moves us.

If you know a song that your giftee loves, this wall art is a thoughtful gift. It presents the song lyrics as a vinyl record, and the whole design is minimal and tasteful enough to fit most interior decor looks. You also get several size and frame options to choose from.

For guitar players, pick up one of these customizable straps. They’re made using soft calf leather, and your message is engraved (instead of painted or printed on). Besides the actual message, you’ll be able to choose the font and the position, and you can even add a photo.

An easy, affordable gift for music lovers (or white elephant parties) is these cute record coasters. They’re made of vinyl to feel like the real thing and feature unique album covers on each one in the pack of six.

Sonos’ new Play speaker bridges the gap between a home device and a portable, offering great sound everywhere. This makes it ideal for college students and recent grads setting up a first apartment.

One of the best pairs of headphones to come out this past year is Technics’ A1000. The cans are based around an advanced 40mm Magnetic Fluid Driver that produces the kind of crisp, natural sound audiophiles will appreciate.

A mini karaoke machine like this one can turn any hangout into a party. The little machine includes two microphones and surprisingly loud sound for its size.

If you’re shopping for a music lover who’s also a sports fan, this summer’s Grateful Dead x Knicks merch is a slam dunk.

A subscription to Fender Play is a great choice for aspiring guitar players. The online learning platform helps beginner and intermediate players improve through high-quality, bite-size videos and exercises.

A subscription to the authority on music and culture is a great gift for anyone. And no, we’re not biased.

Vinyl has been back for a while now, and brands are putting out some very cool record players, including ones with reasonable price tags. Our favorite budget-friendly record player — which also makes a great gift — is this unique Sound Burger from Audio-Technica.

CD players also made a comeback recently. Luckily for us gifters, there are some very fun, affordable options out there, like this colorful player from Gueray.

If your giftee plays music at home (especially drums) or just likes their speakers loud, these acoustic wall panels will let them do it without disturbing others.

One of the best music-book releases of the year was Liza Minnelli’s autobiography, Kids, Wait Till You Hear This! The book gives readers a look at the EGOT winner’s life and career, including her Hollywood upbringing and reflections at nearly 80 years old.

Diogo Dalot has once again been linked with a potential return to AC Milan, where he spent the 2020-21 season on loan.

Calciomercato.com report that Roma’s visit to face Manchester United at Old Trafford will not only be an opportunity for the Giallorossi to face and perhaps erase the ghosts of the past, but also to watch someone up close who could be a name for the not-too-distant future: Diogo Dalot.

Read SempreMilan ad-free and get access to exclusive news. Click here for a free trial!

The Portuguese full-back’s contract is expiring at the end of next season and although he is happy in Manchester, many clubs are beginning to ask his entourage for details.

Roma were one step away from landing the defender in the summer of 2021 when the Portuguese coach José Mourinho was on the bench. The negotiations dragged on for a long time, but in the end it didn’t go through.

The reason ended up being quite simple: the Red Devils never agreed to a loan with option to buy formula while Roma – who were still severely limited by Financial Fair Play limitations – could not offer anything more.

Dalot knows Italy and Serie A well, and he recently spoke about his loan experience with Milan in an interview with The Player’s Tribune.

“It was one of the most important years of my career. I remember the way the club welcomed me and how welcome I felt in a year that was important to me, one in which I needed to regain my confidence in my football. Milan helped me get back to those levels,” he said.

“Every day, Paolo Maldini was on the sidelines to watch us train. Rain, shine, or cold, he was always there, in a suit and tie. He would say to me: ‘Mark like this’ or ‘Tackle it.’ Other times, he would scold me for trying too hard. ‘You’re an extraordinary player, don’t worry, you’ll see, everything will be fine’.”

As mentioned, Dalot’s deal expires in 2028 but there is the presence of a one-year clause that United can trigger. There are a number of teams monitoring his situation including Inter, Milan and Juventus, who have all enquired in recent weeks.

Nothing concrete has emerged yet, as Dalot happy in Manchester and isn’t thinking about the future. However, if things don’t change, a departure – perhaps even for a low-cost fee – could become a realistic possibility.

With the Rossoneri needing defenders, could they try pounce on a reunion for a versatile player that they and Ruben Amorim know well?

‘The Cat in the Hat’ Review: Bill Hader in a Dr. Seuss Adaptation That Captures the Antic Silliness but Misses the Charm

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

The voice cast also includes Quinta Brunson, Bowen Yang, America Ferrera, Giancarlo Esposito and Matt Berry.

About midway through the new animated adaptation of Dr. Seuss’ classic children’s book The Cat in the Hat, there’s an extended silent, slow-motion gag consisting of little more than a fish slowly swimming from one end of the wide screen to the other. It lasts for around 40 seconds and gets funnier as it goes along, with the lack of sound only adding to the mirth.

Take it in, because the wonderfully subtle sequence offers a much-needed respite from the otherwise unrelenting visual and narrative mayhem of the film, which captures the antic silliness of Dr. Seuss without the charm. There are some funny moments along the way, to be sure, of the necessarily juvenile kind (a lengthy bit involves confusion over the expressions “too far” and “toot fart”). Related Stories Beyond the Book 'The Cat in the Hat' Is Back: How the Seuss Icon's First Animated Feature Will Kick Off a New Era Movies Giancarlo Esposito and John Turturro Hated Each Other on 'Do the Right Thing.' Now They Break Character to Cry Mid-Scene The Cat in the Hat The Bottom Line Better than the live-action version, but not by much. Release date: Friday, November 6Cast: Bill Hader, Mayabella Sage McCrilliss, Johnny Haemmerle, Quinta Brunson, Ryan Gaffney, Bowen Yang, Giancarlo Esposito, America Ferrera, Xochitl Gomez, Tiago Martinez, Asha Marie, Paula Pell, Matt Berry, Titus BurgessDirectors-screenwriters: Erica Rivinoja, Alessandro Corloni Rated PG, 1 hour 47 minutes

But the film, while an improvement over the misbegotten 2003 live-action version starring Mike Myers, proves more taxing than enjoyable. It works well enough as a diverting animated effort geared to the youngest kids, and will eventually fulfill its role as streaming babysitter. But it could and should have been a whole lot more.

Bill Hader voices the title character and does as well as you would expect. But the true star of the voice cast turns out to be supporting player Matt Berry as pet goldfish Waffles, who observes the chaos around him with a hilariously droll, deadpan sophistication that produces the film’s biggest laughs even as he runs the risk of becoming a sushi meal.

More of a sequel to the original story than a true adaptation, the film, which begins with a frenetic musical number, presents the Cat not as an independent operator but rather a beleaguered employee of the “Institution of Imagination and Inspiration.” His latest assignment is to cheer up young siblings Gabby (Xochitl Gomez, The Baby-Sitters Club) and Sebastian (Tiago Martinez), whose single mother (an underused America Ferrera) has just moved the family to a new town, much to their dismay.

There’s a lot riding on the Cat, since his exasperated boss Mr. Hoogeboom (Giancarlo Esposito, having fun) threatens to fire him if he doesn’t get the job right. He does enjoy the support of his HR director friend Sherri (a warm Quinta Brunson), but is also forced to deal with a grumpy, self-important intern (a very funny Bowen Yang).

The Cat shows up in the children’s home and whisks them off for a series of chaotic adventures in which they wind up dealing with a variety of fantastical figures, including a marching band made up of musical instruments, several frightening monsters, and, of course, the Things, who have expanded from a duo to multitudes. Communicating entirely through percussive movements and sounds and enough slaps to make the Three Stooges envious, the wild-haired creatures mainly seem to be auditioning for their own big-screen spin-off to compete with the Minions.

Directors-screenwriters Erica Rivinoja (Borat Subsequent Moviefilm, South Park) and Alessandro Carloni (the Kung Fu Panda films) throw a lot — indeed too much — into the mix, with the storyline lapsing into confusion and tonal muddiness. They also employ several different animation styles, including black-and-white imagery early on, to varying degrees of effectiveness. But it’s safe to say that none of it is as visually entrancing as the 1966 hand-drawn How the Grinch Stole Christmas! TV special.

Hader delivers a suitably manic comic performance, but his hard-working efforts aren’t enough to enliven the pedestrian storyline, which unsurprisingly delivers the sort of emotional, inspirational messages toward the end that seem designed to make up for all the preceding anarchy. It doesn’t help that some of the characters are more annoying than endearing, including the unhappy, whiny Gabby and a nastily stern landlady (a lively Paula Pell) who has a redemption arc before the conclusion.

The Cat in the Hat, reviewed in the style of Dr. Seuss

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Ever since I was a small Who, I have loved Dr. Seuss.

His Sneetches, the Lorax, Horton — the joy they induce!

I gobbled up books, shorts, and feature films, too.

I giggled, sang along, and even cried a boo-hoo!

But one of his heroes had a movie fall flat,

That rascally sharp dresser, the Cat in the Hat.

In 2003, Mike Myers tried in live-action.

Audiences marveled at what the duck happened.

Now comes an animated The Cat in the Hat.

Voiced by Bill Hader, this feline is fun.

He brings with him his Things One, Two, and beyond,

Plus a goldfish who wags a fin at things done wrong.

But the focus of this kid's movie is odd:

He works in the business of bringing kids joy,

They've just moved and are feeling quite sad,

But it's not enough to make them feel glad.

Of how the Cat brings mirth to kids near and far.

If that sounds convoluted, oh, just you wait.

This story gets overcrowded to a terrible state.

A mother-daughter squabble kicks off the action,

Then a MacGuffin makes for a time-travel distraction.

Erica Rivinoja and Alessandro Carloni direct and write.

Their animation is warm, delicately textured, and bright.

The voice cast has performers spirited and merry,

Like Quinta Brunson, Xochitl Gomez, and Matt Berry.

Sure, there's fun to be had in this Cat's appearance,

But too many fart jokes hurt the experience.

With an overstuffed plot and action chaotic,

The movie's message feels half-assed and quixotic.

Hader's The Cat In The Hat is certainly better than Myers'.

But so is plucking out my nose hairs with needle-nose pliers.

In the end, I can't cheer this cartoon's feline joker.

Best I can say is that this movie is mediocre.

Windows 11 is now ‘up to 40 percent faster’ on PCs with 8GB of RAM

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

A new Windows 11 update is improving memory usage and performance.

A new Windows 11 update is improving memory usage and performance.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Microsoft has been doing a lot of work to improve memory usage and increase performance on Windows 11 PCs over the past year. Last month the company revealed Windows 11 is now “more responsive” and runs better on PCs with 8GB of RAM, and now the company is releasing a new update today that includes further memory improvements.

“On 8 GB PCs, Windows is now up to 20 percent more responsive, and multitasking is up to 40 percent faster in our lab test,” says Windows chief Pavan Davuluri. One of the key ways Microsoft has achieved better performance on PCs with 8GB of RAM is through compression.

“As a result, Windows can now keep up to 2 GB more of your active data in memory instead of moving it to slower storage, so an app or tab you left open is less likely to reload when you return,” says Davuluri. This compression helps Windows avoid writing data to a virtual memory paging file on a disk, slowing down apps and performance.

Microsoft is also making improvements across Windows, Edge, and Microsoft 365 apps that reduce “committed-memory growth by up to 900 MB.” Memory improvements in Word, PowerPoint, Excel, and Outlook alone add up to 360MB of RAM savings. Windows 11 no longer loads widgets on a PC with 8GB of RAM until you open the widgets panel, saving around 200MB of RAM. Inactive tabs in Edge now sleep after 15 minutes, reducing memory consumption further.

While Microsoft is shipping an update to Windows 11 with these memory improvements, the company is still working on improving RAM usage in Windows in the coming months. Memory compression will get “smarter and more efficient” over the next few months, and Windows features and apps will share more common parts like widgets and search sharing a single web engine. Microsoft is also working with PC makers and chipmakers to optimize how much memory a PC needs to reserve for its hardware, enabling more for Windows.

Xbox’s smallest-ever controller is surprisingly comfortable

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

The Nano controller is designed for young people, anyone with smaller hands, and gaming on the go.

The Nano controller is designed for young people, anyone with smaller hands, and gaming on the go.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Even though I knew Microsoft’s new Xbox Nano controller was tiny, I still gasped when I unboxed it a few days ago. The compact controller is small enough to slip into a pocket unnoticed, and it has been designed specifically for younger people, or for anyone with smaller hands.

I’m not young, but I do have small hands, and I’ve been surprised at how comfortable the Nano is during gaming sessions. At just over 5 inches long, almost everything on this controller has been shrunk down somehow. The ABXY buttons are ever so slightly smaller than a regular Xbox controller, and the thumbsticks feel like miniature versions. Microsoft hasn’t changed the D-pad, though, or the size of the glowing Xbox button at the center of the controller.

The big difference I noticed with the Nano over a regular Xbox controller was the size of the triggers. While they are fully analog and pressure sensitive, they have more of a click when you bottom them out compared with the thud on a bigger Xbox controller. They’re noticeably smaller, as you’d expect, and the bumper buttons above the triggers feel less clicky than the regular ones.

I thought the Nano controller would feel awkward after a few hours of gaming, but the grips really help with comfort levels — a lot of smaller controllers sacrifice grips to keep the controller thin and lightweight. It does mean the Nano doesn’t sit flush on a table, but at 101 grams (0.22 pounds) it’s still really lightweight.

I can’t speak for those with bigger hands, but I’d expect the Nano controller won’t be well-suited to everyone. The size makes it ideal for young children, though. I tried to get my 1-year-old son to try the Nano, and he responded with a smile, so I guess he likes it.

I haven’t had enough time to get a full idea of battery life yet, but it seems to drain around 10 percent in around 30 to 40 minutes. That suggests a lot less battery life than the 30 hours you’d get on a rechargeable pack for the larger Xbox wireless controller. Thankfully, the Nano charges quickly, and I found I could get from 30 percent to 60 percent charge in less than 10 minutes. You can plug in a USB-C charger to top up the battery and carry on playing while you charge.

The only thing missing on the Nano controller that I would have loved is vibration support. I assume it’s not there because of an impact on battery life, because even the smaller Nintendo Joy-Con controllers have rumble support.

The Xbox Nano controller will be available on November 4th, priced at $49.99.

Levi’s claymation holiday campaign celebrates handmade craft amid AI bloat

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Let Marketing Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

“Let’s Get Fuzzy” is centered on a 60-second spot that required four hours of filming for each second of animation.

Levi’s is taking inspiration from a 30-year-old campaign for its holiday effort. “Let’s Get Fuzzy” serves as a call back to the 1995 ad “Clayman,” the brand’s first global commercial and the first full-length spot completely shot in claymation, according to the brand. The brand’s decision to leverage claymation — which is considered to be incredibly labor intensive — could be welcomed by consumers, the majority of whom are growing tired of AI-generated content, particularly in advertising.

The 60-second video opens on a remote cabin in a snow-covered wilderness where friends are enjoying a holiday gathering. The night takes a turn as a ferocious yeti named Gus tries to break into the cabin before successfully running through the wall. Right when all hope seems to be lost, one of the characters throws a red sweater at the yeti in an apparent act of self defense. However, the sweater fits Gus perfectly, stopping his rampage and leading him to join the party as a welcomed guest.

The campaign, which launched two days after Halloween, meshes holiday aesthetics with vibes of the spooky season with its framing and soundtrack choice, akin to cult classics like “Dead Snow.” The video required four hours of filming for each second of animation, and the set and characters were made by hand, according to press details. No AI was used in the filming process, per the brand.

The collection is available via the brand’s website and app, as well as at select retail locations.

“Our Levi’s Let’s Get Fuzzy campaign was an opportunity to experiment with immersive and tactile world-building,” said Kenny Mitchell, global CMO at Levi’s, in press materials. “Anchored by a film created entirely with stop-motion claymation, it’s also the celebration of the craft and human creativity synonymous with the Levi’s brand.”

Generative AI has become an increasingly common tool for marketers as the industry looks to cut costs and meet the demands of an ever-evolving cultural landscape. However, cost savings and quick turnaround times come at a price. Consumers routinely find AI creative as “annoying,” “boring” and often “confusing,” according to a 2024 study by NielsenIQ. Half of consumers prefer brands that don’t use generative AI in advertising and other forward-facing messaging, per a 2026 Gartner study.

Levi’s isn’t the only company looking to use handcrafted creatives this holiday season. E.l.f. and Prada also launched handmade, stop-motion style holiday campaigns. Retail giants Walmart and Target both launched their campaigns before Halloween. The holiday shopping window has continued to get earlier as consumers hunt for the best value amid budgetary pressure.

Parent Levi Strauss & Co. reported a third-quarter revenue of $1.6 billion, a 4% year-over-year increase, according to an earnings release. Michelle Gass, CEO, president and director of Levi Strauss, noted on an earnings call that there has been less brand-building moments this year and that the brand’s back-to-school campaign did not generate the anticipated demand and traffic. The company plans to deepen its marketing investments and place more of a focus on mid-funnel marketing, according to Gass.

The Senate permitting bill holds promise but needs amendments

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Targeted changes can minimize the most negative consequences of the permitting bill’s overreaching provisions, writes former FERC Chairman Mark Christie.

Mark Christie is a former chairman of the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission and former chairman of the Virginia State Corporation Commission, and is the director of the William & Mary Law School Center for Energy Law and Policy.

Every true compromise in a polarized legislature contains candy for each side, but also requires each side to swallow some hairballs. In the Senate permitting reform bill (SB 5653), the true permitting reforms hold a welcome potential to get needed infrastructure built faster. The transmission provisions, however — which are not permitting reforms at all — contain some provisions that are indigestible if you care about rising consumer costs and respect for historic state authority over utilities.

The bill’s best transmission provisions seek to ensure that large-load customers pay the full costs they impose on the grid. The worst transmission provisions, however, can be fixed with the amendments described below. Before considering these amendments, it is important to understand that the Federal Power Act for nearly a century has recognized a clear and logical division between state and federal authority over the power grid: The states have authority over the siting and permitting of all physical assets within their borders, including generation and transmission. The Federal Energy Regulatory Commission has authority over the rates for interstate transmission services and wholesale power sales.

The current Senate bill blows up this century-old balance of authority by giving FERC the sweeping power to order a transmission line built in any state, without regard to the state’s own decision on the project or the opposition of the state’s residents who will be directly affected. Congress has enacted so-called “backstop siting authority” before, most recently in the Infrastructure Investment and Jobs Act in 2021, but Congress strictly limited the geographic reach of FERC’s backstop authority to narrow geographic corridors established by the U.S. Department of Energy and only after DOE made a finding that the national interest required the establishment of each corridor.

The Senate bill contains no geographic limits on FERC’s permitting power. It expands FERC’s authority to override the states to every square inch of the country, a breathtaking expansion of federal power at the expense of a core state function: the permitting and construction of utility assets within their own borders, projects that could have huge impacts on the lives of their residents.

Fortunately, targeted amendments can minimize the most negative consequences of the bill’s overreaching provisions. Here are three recommended amendments:

The bill allows FERC to order the construction of transmission lines based on a nebulous “public interest” standard. Such a standard is a blank check for FERC to approve trillions of dollars’ worth of transmission lines for whatever reason a presidential administration finds is in the “public interest,” as long as FERC adds general findings of some reliability improvements or consumer benefits, which can be claimed for every transmission line ever proposed. In the short term, FERC could use the bill’s grant of new authority to order transmission lines built to serve hyperscale data center development, a Trump administration priority. A future FERC could find that the public interest requires trillions of dollars of new transmission to meet “Net Zero” climate goals.

So my first recommended amendment is to remove the “public interest” and other general criteria, and instead require that FERC must find that the proposed line is the optimal solution to meet a proven reliability need at just and reasonable rates, compared to alternative solutions.

My second amendment addresses the critical role of states in cost allocation. FERC Order 1920 guaranteed to states in a multi-state region the right to reach an agreement on cost allocation for regional transmission projects and have that agreement submitted to FERC. The Senate bill should add an explicit guarantee that for regional transmission projects the states in each region have the right to agree on cost allocation and FERC shall presume such state agreement is just and reasonable.

My third amendment concerns one of the most onerous and frankly insulting provisions in the Senate bill. When we at FERC implemented the Infrastructure Investment and Jobs Act’s backstop siting authority, we gave each affected state one full year to conduct its permitting proceeding before FERC would start its own backstop proceeding. That allowed the state to develop a factual record and issue a decision within a reasonable period of one year.

The Senate bill allows FERC to start its backstop proceeding simultaneously with the state proceeding. That effectively renders the state proceeding meaningless, and states will perceive it exactly that way. Why should a state spend its time and limited resources on its proceeding when FERC is already doing its own? Any evidentiary record developed in the state proceeding may not even be available to FERC before it overrides the state. This provision in the Senate bill is nakedly punitive and overreaching and should be amended to give the states one full year to act before FERC can begin its own backstop proceeding.

Even if you don’t care about respecting state authority, you should care about the bill’s impact on consumers already struggling to pay their power bills. Over the past decade, transmission has been one of the fastest-growing drivers of power bill increases. The Senate bill as drafted could result in literally trillions of dollars of new transmission being ordered by FERC to serve whatever policy goals a president wants. These costs will flow right into consumers’ monthly bills.

Every compromise offers candy and hairballs. With these amendments, the bill can be made a lot sweeter.

Get the free daily newsletter read by industry experts

PJM had planned to accept offers for backstop power supplies from Sept. 30 through Oct. 21 after falling short of its reserve margin targets. But FERC only partially approved its plan, zeroing in on cost allocation concerns.

Major work by regional planners is focused on flexible access to existing transmission. Some say greater emphasis on advanced transmission technologies can quickly and cost-effectively create room for new large loads.

Afterwords – Marvel’s Wolverine

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

We interviewed game director Mike Daly after the launch of Insomniac’s recent Wolverine game to get his take on what makes Wolverine and Spidey different to work on, his thoughts on breadcrumb trails, and why Logan can’t ever seem to get the girl.

Insomniac had extensive experience working on well-known superhero characters before starting on Wolverine. Early on, what traits or qualities of Logan as a story character and playable avatar did you know you wanted to focus on, distinct from the Spider-Man games and character?

From a story perspective, we wanted Logan to be a man with centuries of experiences that he can’t remember, creating a mix of being worn, wizened, and cynical. His resilience makes him fearless, which comes through in his candid manner, and he can’t ignore his sense of justice, as much as he would like to.

For gameplay, it was important that players felt the difference made by his healing factor, claws, berserker rage, animalistic strength, enhanced senses, his adamantium skeleton, and corresponding weight.

In both story and gameplay, emotionally and physically, Wolverine is a hero who gets hurt and feels that pain deeply but always gets back up again and presses on. This dogged resilience was really important to us and a central theme of story and gameplay.

Wolverine is more of a linear adventure than the open-world exploration of a big city seen in Spidey. In my playthrough, that added to the sense of relentless pacing that characterizes Wolverine as a fictional lead. Was that part of the thinking behind why the game was built that way, or was the more linear path there for other reasons?

Ultimately, we made a game suited for a Wolverine story that had comic book pacing – action and meaningful story development on every page.

We experimented with different structures and stories, evaluating from the perspective of: “Is this the best way to do Wolverine justice?” As the various things we tried didn’t feel right, we had to challenge our assumptions and look to our favorite Wolverine stories for inspiration, which led us to where we ended up – a story with momentum in which every beat felt authentic to the character.

Scenes and gameplay sequences that might have been fine in other games but didn’t strongly evoke Wolverine undermined the consistency of the fantasy we were trying to create, so we tried to keep things very focused.

After the game’s release, there was an active online conversation about some of the breadcrumb-trail guidance the game offers to lead you to pickups, or to mark the continued golden path of progression. What was the thinking internally about that design decision, and your take on the debate after the game launched?

Our thinking was that it was essential to make the player feel like they were empowered by Logan’s enhanced senses, a canonical mutant ability, enabling players to do things they could not with other characters. We wanted to weave that power into as many parts of the game as we could, so that if Logan could smell or hear something relevant, the player could see it too.

For collectibles, we wanted players to enjoy seeking out and spotting senses cues, enabling you to find things that would have been obscure to other characters. Using the cues, players can cover a lot of ground without feeling the need to scour, maintaining story and action momentum.

After the game launched, we saw feedback from players that interpreted senses as abstract designer guidance, along with a desire for options in how they approached exploration and discovery. Fortunately, we have been able to incorporate this feedback from players in our post-launch patches. One aspect we’ve improved is the scent trail’s organic presentation, so they give a stronger impression that they belong in the world rather than being an abstraction. For players that want to do more manual exploration, we’ve added options to remove the sense cues for optional collectibles, and one to make scent trails dimmer until the player requests a hint.

Ultimately, enhanced senses are still essential to creating an authentic Wolverine, and that’s still reflected in the cinematic and environmental storytelling of the game. I think it’s great that on top of that, players now have more flexibility in how they experience them.

As a longtime comics fan, I found Logan’s combat system especially exciting, particularly for the relentless forward momentum it provided. What do you think were some of the keys to nailing that dynamic, as distinct from other high-quality third-person action games?

There were a few key elements we wanted to get right - Logan’s resilience, rage, claw feel, and overall lethality.

For resilience, Logan’s healing factor needed to feel important for survival - a prominent element of the system. A natural starting point there was to try continual regeneration so you could outlast enemies, but that quickly fell apart as it incentivized the player to slow the pace of combat or even retreat for maximum advantage. Even though it was incredibly effective at enabling survival, it was also hard to notice by the player so it didn’t feel prominent.

The key to solving this problem was making a connection between rage and healing factor. The Last Stand mechanic is a healing surge that can happen when you lose all your health, themed as using adrenaline to keep Logan’s heart beating. This incentivized aggression and made a space for an up-close look at visceral healing.

Speaking of rage, it’s the backbone of the combat system where being an indicator of momentum is key. At the low end of rage, you don’t have the safety net of Last Stand, so your decision-making naturally biases towards survival; ensuring you prioritize threat assessment and defense. As you climb into the middle tier, you have the leeway to push your luck for longer combos or barrages that let you build up rage and get kills faster. Finally, you get to rage tier 3, where the quick decay of rage makes your rate of kills the top priority, so you might prefer to attack groups over ranged stragglers just for the improved kill density. Beyond the mechanics, we change the presentation of combat, especially through Logan’s animations and exertion to reflect his state of mind. The presentation and systemic incentives drive the player into a frenzy, just like Wolverine.

We invested a lot in the details of claw feel to make sure it was distinct from general game attacks. Claws shred, so we added locational body damage to our enemies to reflect that, along with a dynamic blood system where it globs together as it is spilled, splatters on contact with characters or the environment, drips down the walls, diffuses in the water, and other details. Many kills throughout combat are punctuated with custom animations with cinematic camera work that pushes in and frames the puncturing of claws into the body for a strong impression of the connection and weight of the puncture. Severing limbs on these attacks is an occasional reminder of how sharp Logan’s adamantium claws are. Reinforcing this is top-notch sound and haptics design, so each hit registers.

The last aspect I’ll touch on is lethality, the rate at which Wolverine is able to dispatch enemies and vice versa. We found that fights where enemies were more technical and could survive a lot undermined what players expected to experience as Wolverine, which was aggressively and lethally tearing through a lot of enemies. On the other hand, combat was less exciting if the enemies didn’t feel like a serious and deadly threat, and you didn’t feel like Wolverine if a little bit of caution could enable you to defend perfectly and avoid all damage.

These aspects led us towards encounters where you confronted large groups of enemies that were highly aggressive, to the point of feeling unfair. Enemies can’t believably go toe-to-toe with Wolverine alone, even world-class mercenaries, so their strategy is in group coordination, which creates a counterweight to Wolverine’s own rage-based momentum.

Marvel’s Wolverine draws in a number of characters from across the X-Men comic mythology, but notably makes no mention of some foundational figures of that fiction – Professor Xavier and Magneto. That, in turn, leads to dramatically different lives for characters like Jean Grey or Logan. Is the narrative you crafted an intentional exploration of a world where those figures weren’t present in the early days of mutant emergence?

I would say it was more of an exploration of the character than the world. We wanted a story where Logan is pulled into conflict not because he’s a crusader, but because there’s no other option. There are no X-Men to help. His growth as a person should be a challenging journey, which could go unearned if he was surrounded by wise, supportive, heroic characters pushing him in the right direction. In contrast, his dynamic with the members of Team X evokes his struggle. The adversity to personal growth is what makes it interesting.

Jean does end up being the person that pushes him in the right direction, and that works because they can relate to each other, having had similar struggles.

In several versions of Marvel storytelling, Jean and Logan share a special bond, but Jean ultimately chooses Scott Summers as a partner. But even without that barrier in your story, Logan still hits some roadblocks to love. Is Wolverine fundamentally a better character when he just can’t get the girl?

The way I would put it is that a Wolverine story is fundamentally better when you empathize with Logan’s pain and admire him for pressing on despite it. That goes for his emotional pain as much as physical. He’s a tragic character in this regard – all he wants is to be at peace but can never find it for long.

Wolverine gets to take on a lot of the “heavy hitters” of his comic mythology in different boss fights throughout the game. When you began development, do you recall who the very first character was that you decided he simply had to fight in your game?

Sabretooth. No character is a better foil for Logan. His inner turmoil and chaotic behavior helps illustrate Logan’s own inner beast and his struggle to resist it. Sabretooth’s strength and mutant powers also provide a mirror to Logan in combat, and his savagery creates shocking moments that leave an impression. We knew an epic confrontation with Sabretooth was going to be a thing from the start.
